The dispatch tax: Python overhead vs. GPU reality

ML Frameworks

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why does the framework silently constrain every decision that comes after it?

Model equations do not execute themselves. A framework translates an architecture into work that hardware can run, and that translation is not neutral. It decides when operations execute, what intermediate state training retains, how memory is allocated, and which deployment targets can use the resulting artifact. These choices shape more than speed. They also determine what can be debugged directly, what the compiler can optimize, and whether the same model survives the path from a research environment to a production runtime. The same model expressed through two execution paths can differ in speed or memory use by several times, a gap that no amount of model tuning recovers when the framework hides the structure a compiler needs. The abstractions that make experimentation convenient therefore create long-lived commitments. Once a project accumulates checkpoints, modules, data loaders, serving formats, and team expertise around a framework, changing it becomes a system migration rather than a library substitution, measured in engineer-months rather than afternoons. A framework well suited to rapid exploration can expose too little structure for efficient deployment, while a constrained execution path can sacrifice the flexibility needed during discovery. Because execution, differentiation, and abstraction meet in the same software layer, a local convenience can become a downstream performance or portability constraint. The engineering question is not which framework is universally best, but which trade-offs the workload, team, and target machine can afford to make fixed. Understanding frameworks is therefore about reasoning through those system choices rather than learning transient API syntax, which changes with each release while the underlying trade-offs persist. In D·A·M terms, the framework mediates algorithm-machine co-design by turning algorithmic intent and data representation into executable work under machine constraints.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain how frameworks mediate algorithm-machine co-design through execution, differentiation, and hardware abstraction
  • Compare execution strategies using dispatch overhead, compilation cost, and deployment constraints
  • Analyze backward saved-state requirements and explain the storage and recomputation consequences of a supplied activation-checkpointing policy
  • Implement module abstraction patterns for parameter discovery, mode behavior, serialization, and hooks
  • Calculate training-step FLOPs, memory traffic, and dispatch overhead to identify fusion and layout bottlenecks
  • Select TensorFlow, PyTorch, JAX, or edge runtimes based on model, hardware, team, and deployment requirements

Three Framework Problems

Network Architectures ended by treating architecture selection as a contract with the hardware. A convolutional neural network (CNN) commits the system to spatial locality and weight reuse, and a transformer commits it to attention, matrix multiplications, activation state, and memory traffic. Those commitments fix what work exists. The framework is the layer that decides how that work reaches the machine. A few calls such as logits = model(tokens), loss = criterion(logits, targets), and loss.backward() hide billions of floating-point operations across memory hierarchies, gradients through millions of parameters via automatic differentiation, thousands of GPU kernel launches, and gigabytes of intermediate state. The API looks simple because the framework is acting as a compiler for the silicon contract.

The gap between what and how is wide. A transformer’s attention mechanism requires coordinating computation across memory hierarchies and accelerator cores in patterns where naive implementations can run one to two orders of magnitude slower than optimized ones because of uncoalesced memory accesses (scattered reads the hardware cannot merge into wide memory transactions) and repeated round-trips to high-bandwidth memory (HBM). Implementing these operations from scratch for every model would make deep learning economically infeasible. ML frameworks exist to bridge this gap by lowering abstract model graphs into hardware-aligned execution pipelines that maximize compute unit utilization while minimizing off-chip memory traffic.

A framework is to machine learning what a compiler is to traditional programming. A C compiler translates human-readable code into optimized machine instructions, managing register allocation, instruction scheduling, and memory layout. An ML framework translates high-level model definitions into hardware-specific execution plans, managing kernel fusion, memory reuse, and device placement. The correspondence is operational rather than figurative: modern frameworks incorporate optimizing compilers (such as Accelerated Linear Algebra (XLA) or TorchInductor) that lower graph representations directly to target machine code.

Every ML framework, regardless of API or design philosophy, must solve three core problems. The first is the execution problem: deciding when and how computation runs. A framework can execute operations immediately as written (eager execution1) or first construct a computational graph2 (a record of operations and their data dependencies, developed in section 1.3.2) to optimize before launching device kernels (graph execution). This choice determines the fundamental trade-off between interactive debugging and whole-program compiler optimization.

1 Eager execution: This mode executes each operation immediately, which enables direct debugging with standard tools but sacrifices the global view needed for graph-level optimizations. Without capturing a larger region of computation, an eager runtime cannot fuse across that region or preplan its memory, leaving workload-dependent optimization opportunities for compilers such as torch.compile.

2 Computational graph: Optimizing before executing is the design choice that separates graph execution from eager execution. Capturing the program as a data structure lets a framework fuse compatible operations into fewer GPU kernels before execution, reducing launch overhead and intermediate memory traffic. The engineering cost of this visibility is that the executed program differs from the source code, making debugging harder, a trade-off every graph-based framework must justify against the performance gain.

With an execution strategy in place, the framework must solve the differentiation problem: computing gradients automatically. Because gradient-based training requires derivatives of a scalar loss with respect to millions or billions of parameters, manual calculus is impossible at scale. Frameworks implement automatic differentiation by registering derivative rules across supported operator compositions, chaining vector-Jacobian products while managing numerical precision and the memory overhead of intermediate activations retained for backward passes.

↩︎ Reverse-mode automatic differentiation and computational graph traversal for gradient computation are established in Gradient computation and backpropagation.

Tillet, Philippe, H. T. Kung, and David Cox. 2019. “Triton: An Intermediate Language and Compiler for Tiled Neural Network Computations.” Proceedings of the 3rd ACM SIGPLAN International Workshop on Machine Learning and Programming Languages, 10–19. https://doi.org/10.1145/3315508.3329973.

The third problem is hardware abstraction: targeting diverse hardware from a single interface. The same model definition should be expressible across CPUs, GPUs, Tensor Processing Units (TPUs), and mobile accelerators, even though each target presents distinct memory hierarchies, instruction sets, and interconnect bandwidths. Within the GPU domain, framework ecosystems increasingly expose custom-kernel languages such as Triton (Tillet et al. 2019), allowing developers to write high-performance, block-level kernels without hand-crafting low-level CUDA or device assembly.

These three problems are tightly coupled: the execution model dictates when differentiation data structures are recorded, while the hardware abstraction layer determines which graph transformations can be lowered to silicon. Unifying execution, differentiation, and hardware abstraction under physical machine constraints requires evaluating frameworks through the governing equation of machine learning performance.

Systems Perspective 7.1: The ML compiler
In the context of the iron law of ML systems (principle 3), a framework is a compiler for the silicon contract (principle 4).

The “source code” is the model architecture (the \(\Operations\) term). The framework’s job is to take this high-level mathematical specification and compile it into a series of hardware-specific kernel launches that:

  1. Minimize data movement \((\DataVolume)\) through techniques such as kernel fusion.
  2. Maximize utilization \((\EfficiencyHw)\) by matching operations to specialized hardware units such as Tensor Cores.
  3. Minimize overhead \((\Latency)\) through efficient asynchronous dispatch and graph capture.

Choosing a framework therefore also chooses the compiler that determines hardware efficiency. Read against the three framework problems, execution strategy bears most directly on \(\Latency\), differentiation on \(\DataVolume\) (and on the operation count \(\Operations\)), and abstraction on \(\EfficiencyHw\). These are emphases rather than exclusive assignments, since fusion, for example, cuts both launches and traffic, and the chapter uses this one mapping throughout. The formal definition below makes that compiler role precise and separates frameworks from numerical libraries such as NumPy.

Definition 7.1: Machine learning frameworks

Machine learning frameworks are software systems that translate high-level mathematical model definitions into hardware-optimized execution plans by managing the computational graph, automatic differentiation, kernel dispatch, and memory allocation across the hardware hierarchy.

  1. Significance: Frameworks shape every iron law term that the model itself does not fix: the bytes moved \((\DataVolume)\), the hardware efficiency achieved \((\EfficiencyHw)\), and the fixed overhead paid \((\Latency)\). Compiler-backed kernel fusion, for example, can eliminate writes and subsequent reads of intermediate values between compatible operations. Fusing a matrix multiplication, bias add, and rectified linear unit (ReLU) changes how the same mathematics reaches hardware without changing the model.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a numerical library such as NumPy, which normally evaluates each operation as it is called, an ML framework can capture or defer execution to analyze a larger computational graph and apply cross-operation optimizations: kernel fusion, memory layout transformations, and parallel scheduling. Those optimizations require visibility beyond separately dispatched operations.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that frameworks are interchangeable API wrappers. Framework choice determines which compiler paths are available. PyTorch can recover graph-level optimization from eager code through torch.compile(), while TensorFlow and JAX commonly rely on XLA-backed compilation paths that lower operations to target hardware. Moving from eager execution to a compiler path can change throughput materially, but the gain depends on model structure, shapes, operator support, and hardware.

An ML framework translates logical intent into physical execution under the constraints of the iron law, deciding how to partition computation across memory hierarchies, when to trade numerical precision for throughput, and how to schedule operations so that whichever term dominates (data movement \(\DataVolume\), arithmetic computation, or launch latency \(\Latency\)) is minimized. The framework is where the governing physics developed throughout this book becomes executable code.

Automating this machinery—recording operation graphs, allocating gradient buffers, traversing dependencies in reverse, and dispatching hardware kernels—did not happen all at once. Execution, differentiation, and abstraction emerged sequentially as responses to scaling bottlenecks in earlier numerical tools, creating a four-decade historical progression from hand-tuned assembly routines to compiled graph runtimes.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team reports that their model executes correctly on CPU but produces mismatched tensor shapes and silent numerical corruption when switched to a GPU backend because some operators silently default to a different memory layout (such as NCHW versus NHWC). Which of the three fundamental framework problems does this failure most directly expose?

    1. The hardware abstraction problem, because one unified model interface must preserve consistent semantic behavior, memory layouts, and numerical contracts across diverse hardware backends
    2. The execution problem, because the operators were evaluated eagerly instead of being captured into a static graph
    3. The differentiation problem, because the backward pass failed to propagate gradients through non-contiguous strides
    4. A data engineering pipeline defect unrelated to framework runtime responsibilities

  2. Explain how viewing an ML framework as a compiler for the silicon contract—rather than merely a numerical library like NumPy—changes an engineer’s expectations regarding framework selection and optimization under the systems iron law.

  3. True or False: Two frameworks that expose nearly identical user-facing Python tensor APIs and target the same GPU hardware will necessarily provide equivalent graph-level operator fusion and ahead-of-time compilation capabilities.

  4. An engineering organization chose a specialized research framework for rapid prototyping, only to discover later that the framework lacks export paths to their production edge accelerators, requiring months of manual re-implementation. Applying the chapter’s infrastructure-commitment principle, what is the key systems insight?

    1. Framework choice is easily reversible because weight arrays can be loaded into any runtime with zero engineering overhead
    2. Framework selection functions as a durable infrastructure commitment whose reversal cost compounds across model checkpoints, serving runtimes, CI/CD pipelines, and hardware access
    3. The primary failure was selecting an overly compact model architecture that failed to saturate edge accelerator memory
    4. Hardware abstraction layers eliminate all differences between training frameworks and production inference engines

The Ladder of Abstraction

In 1979, writing a matrix multiplication in Fortran that used the hardware efficiently required deep knowledge of cache lines, register scheduling, and vector units. By 2016, a single line of Python (torch.matmul(A, W)) could dispatch to a highly optimized implementation without the programmer knowing the details of the silicon. That compression of effort did not happen in one step; it accumulated across four decades of abstraction, each layer solving a bottleneck that made the previous generation impractical for scaling. The result is a ladder of abstraction where each rung automates what the rung below exposed.

The first rung established reusable hardware primitives (1979–1992). The original Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms (BLAS)3 standardized low-level linear-algebra operations (Lawson et al. 1979), while LAPACK4 (Bai et al. 2006) built higher-level numerical factorizations and eigensolvers on top of that foundation. Naive nested loops fail to exploit memory hierarchies: without cache tiling and register blocking, memory bandwidth saturates long before arithmetic units reach peak throughput. BLAS and LAPACK solved this hardware-primitive bottleneck by establishing stable interfaces that let numerical software delegate compute-heavy operations such as matrix multiplication (C = A @ B)5 to vendor libraries tuned for specific cache line sizes, single instruction, multiple data (SIMD) widths, and register files, rather than requiring hand-written assembly for every target architecture.

3 BLAS (basic linear algebra subprograms): The 1979 API specification that forms the bottom rung of the ladder described here; it standardized a fixed set of Fortran-callable vector operations, separating the public routine names from the machine-specific implementation decisions beneath them. Every framework above it inherits the broader version of this bargain: call a standard linear-algebra primitive from any language and let a tuned vendor library target the silicon. For modern general matrix multiply (GEMM) on NVIDIA GPUs, that tuned path is cuBLAS rather than the 1979 BLAS specification itself (NVIDIA 2024a).

NVIDIA. 2024a. cuBLAS: CUDA Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms.
Lawson, Charles L., Richard J. Hanson, David R. Kincaid, and Fred T. Krogh. 1979. “Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms for Fortran Usage.” ACM Transactions on Mathematical Software 5 (3): 308–23. https://doi.org/10.1145/355841.355847.

4 LAPACK (linear algebra package): Extends BLAS by providing a standard API for higher-level routines (SVD, eigendecomposition, least-squares) that vendors implement with chip-specific code layered on top of fast GEMM kernels. This layered design is the architectural pattern every ML framework inherits: high-level operations delegate downward to hand-tuned primitives, so a vendor-optimized LAPACK call can execute over 10\(\times\) faster than a naive implementation without the framework author writing a single line of hardware-specific code.

Bai, Zhaojun, James Demmel, Jack Dongarra, Julien Langou, and Jenny Wang. 2006. “LAPACK.” In Handbook of Linear Algebra. Chapman; Hall/CRC. https://doi.org/10.1201/9781420010572-75.

5 GEMM: The matrix-matrix primitive behind C = A @ B. Hardware vendors hand-tune GEMM for their specific chips because dense layers, attention projections, and convolution lowering all rely on matrix multiplication, making this one routine a performance floor for many frameworks above it on the ladder. Its high arithmetic intensity makes GEMM the operation most able to approach peak compute throughput, while small or misaligned shapes often fall back to much lower utilization.

6 NumPy (numerical Python): In 2005, Travis Oliphant unified two competing Python array libraries (Numeric and Numarray) into a single package, giving the scientific computing community one BLAS-backed array standard at the moment it needed to scale. The “vectorization” contract this created (write logic in Python, execute loops in C/Fortran via BLAS) became the design template for every ML framework that followed: PyTorch tensors and TensorFlow arrays are direct descendants, extending the same \(\TensorAxisCount\)-dimensional array abstraction to GPUs. Python’s role in ML infrastructure inherits much of its shape from this consolidation decision.

Harris, Charles R., K. Jarrod Millman, Stéfan J. van der Walt, Ralf Gommers, Pauli Virtanen, David Cournapeau, Eric Wieser, et al. 2020. “Array Programming with NumPy.” Nature 585 (7825): 357–62. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-020-2649-2.

The second rung introduced unified array abstractions to unlock developer velocity (2005–2006). NumPy6 addressed the productivity barrier of low-level languages by wrapping BLAS and LAPACK in high-level Python (Harris et al. 2020). Under NumPy’s strided array model, Python manages control flow and tensor metadata (shape, strides, and data type), while the underlying C runtime dispatches vector and matrix loops across contiguous memory buffers. This vectorization contract—orchestrating high-level algorithmic intent in an interpreted language while delegating inner loops to compiled native routines—became the architectural template for scientific and numerical computing.

The third rung automated gradient computation (2007–present). Deep learning frameworks such as Theano,7 TensorFlow (Abadi et al. 2016), and PyTorch (Paszke et al. 2019) solved the scalability limits of manual differentiation. While numerical packages required practitioners to derive and implement analytical backpropagation gradients by hand—a process error-prone and unmanageable for deep networks—modern frameworks turned automatic differentiation over computational graphs into a core runtime primitive. By capturing operations in a directed acyclic graph during the forward pass, these frameworks automatically construct the backward graph to evaluate vector-Jacobian products, transforming the calculus of the chain rule into automated reverse-mode execution. That automation let the bitter lesson operate at the pace of research. Once a new architecture no longer needed a hand-derived backward pass, general methods that scale with computation could be tried as quickly as their forward functions could be written.

7 Theano: Developed at the Montreal Institute for Learning Algorithms (MILA) under Yoshua Bengio starting in 2007, Theano was an early and influential Python framework that compiled symbolic mathematical expressions into optimized CPU and GPU code via computational graphs (Bergstra et al. 2010; Team et al. 2016). It demonstrated that a Python-defined computation graph could be compiled for GPU execution without requiring the researcher to write CUDA code. Mila ended active development of Theano in 2017 (Mila 2026).

Bergstra, James, Olivier Breuleux, Frédéric Bastien, Pascal Lamblin, Razvan Pascanu, Guillaume Desjardins, Joseph Turian, David Warde-Farley, and Yoshua Bengio. 2010. “Theano: A CPU and GPU Math Compiler in Python.” Proceedings of the Python in Science Conference 4: 18–24. https://doi.org/10.25080/majora-92bf1922-003.
Team, The Theano Development, Rami Al-Rfou, Guillaume Alain, Amjad Almahairi, Christof Angermueller, Dzmitry Bahdanau, Nicolas Ballas, et al. 2016. “Theano: A Python Framework for Fast Computation of Mathematical Expressions.” arXiv Preprint.
Mila. 2026. Open Source Software.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, that general methods which exploit growing computation outlast hand-engineered approaches, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

Figure 1 sequences these numerical computing milestones across four decades (1979–2018), charting the shift from assembly-tuned BLAS and LAPACK routines to NumPy vectorization, symbolic graph capture in Theano and TensorFlow, dynamic dispatch in PyTorch, and functional transformation in JAX.

Each generation abstracted away details that consumed engineering effort in the previous one, yet each abstraction introduced new trade-offs. BLAS hid assembly-level optimization but fixed the interface. NumPy hid memory management but required manual differentiation. Modern frameworks hide gradient computation but introduce a choice among execution models. The deeper pattern is that every abstraction hides details by preserving a contract about shape, dtype, device, and sometimes units; when that contract becomes implicit, correct components can still compose into a wrong system.

War Story 7.1: The interface that forgot its units (1999)
Context: NASA’s Mars Climate Orbiter depended on ground software, spacecraft software, navigation teams, and contractor interfaces all agreeing on the physical meaning of exchanged values (Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board 1999).

Mechanism: Ground software supplied by Lockheed Martin computed thruster impulse in English units (pound-force seconds), while JPL navigation software consumed the values assuming SI units (newton-seconds)—a 4.45\(\times\) implicit unit mismatch.

Impact: On September 23, 1999, the spacecraft was lost after entering occultation. Its corrected 57 km periapsis, rather than the planned 226 km, was judged too low for survival.

Response: The board recommended unit-consistency checks, specification audits of transferred data, and stronger end-to-end verification of ground software.

Systems lesson: Framework abstractions are valuable because they carry contracts: shape, dtype, device placement, and physical units. If those contracts are implicit, two pieces of correct code can still compose into a wrong system. ML frameworks hit this whenever tensor memory layouts (such as channels-first NCHW [\(\text{Batch} \times \text{Channels} \times \text{Height} \times \text{Width}\)] vs. channels-last NHWC [\(\text{Batch} \times \text{Height} \times \text{Width} \times \text{Channels}\)]), quantization scale factors, or physical feature units differ across pipeline stages, producing plausible outputs that quietly corrupt downstream inference.

Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board. 1999. Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board Phase i Report. National Aeronautics; Space Administration.

Preventing these silent mismatches requires checking contracts somewhere between writing code and running it. A framework can check them as each operation executes, when the failing line and its intermediate values are directly visible, or before execution, when a captured graph can be validated and optimized as a whole. When those checks and optimizations happen is the execution problem. It is the first framework design choice because runtime dispatch and graph capture determine not only the debugging experience but also what optimizations automatic differentiation and hardware mapping can subsequently exploit.

Self-Check: Question

  1. While NumPy provided high-performance linear algebra by wrapping BLAS in Python, what critical scaling bottleneck did it leave unaddressed that motivated the development of deep learning frameworks such as Theano, TensorFlow, and PyTorch?

    2. Lack of an \(n\)-dimensional array data structure in scientific computing
    3. The requirement for manual gradient derivation and hand-written backpropagation passes for multi-layer neural networks
    4. Inability to run compiled Fortran and C routines through high-level scripting languages

  2. Explain why the relationship between rungs on the ladder of abstraction (such as BLAS/LAPACK, NumPy, and modern deep learning frameworks) is characterized by inheritance rather than replacement.

  3. Order the following historical computing milestones in the evolution of numerical and machine learning software abstractions, from earliest (1979) to most recent (2018):

  4. JAX introduces functional composable transformations and XLA compilation

  5. BLAS standardizes reusable low-level linear algebra primitives

  6. Theano introduces compiled Python computational graphs for GPUs

  7. NumPy establishes Python’s unified \(n\)-dimensional array and vectorization standard

  9. LAPACK extends BLAS with higher-level numerical routines (e.g., SVD, factorizations)

  10. The architectural design pattern established by NumPy, where high-level control logic is written in an expressive interpreted language (such as Python) while inner numerical loops are delegated to compiled C/Fortran libraries, is known as ____.
