Execution Problem

Consider two engineers writing the same neural network. The first debugs interactively, printing tensor shapes after each operation, inspecting intermediate values, and stepping through code with pdb. The second waits 30 seconds for compilation, then watches a model built from many small operations run 3\(\times\) faster while losing that direct, line-by-line view of intermediate state. Both are correct; they have made different choices about the execution problem, the question of whether operations should execute immediately as written or be recorded for later execution. This choice creates a cascade of engineering trade-offs that shape every aspect of framework behavior, from debugging workflows to deployment options to peak hardware utilization.

Why execution strategy matters: The memory wall

The execution strategy of an ML framework is constrained by the memory wall, the widening gap between compute growth and memory-bandwidth growth that equation 2.3 quantifies. Modern accelerators can perform arithmetic far faster than they can fetch data. Element-wise operations such as ReLU use only a tiny fraction of peak compute capacity, not because the hardware is slow, but because they spend nearly all their time waiting for data. The Roofline model formalizes this trade-off, showing exactly when operations are memory bound vs. compute bound.

↩︎ The memory wall and the growth-rate ratio behind it are established in Physical Constraints: Why Paradigms Exist.

The physical root of this imbalance is accelerator chip topography: silicon is partitioned between tiny, ultra-fast on-chip storage (registers and SRAM scratchpads, operating at tens of terabytes per second but holding only tens of megabytes) and large off-chip device memory (High Bandwidth Memory [HBM] or GDDR, holding 40–80 GB but reached across external memory buses with hundreds of cycles of latency). When evaluating an unoptimized sequence of three operations—such as \(\text{LayerNorm} \to \text{Dropout} \to \text{ReLU}\)—a framework dispatches three separate GPU kernels. The first reads inputs from HBM and writes intermediate results back to HBM; the second reads those results and writes back again; the third reads them a final time. Shuttling intermediate tensors across the external bus three times dwarfs the computation time, leaving arithmetic units idle.

The memory wall classifies operations as either compute-bound (limited by arithmetic throughput) or memory-bound (limited by data movement). Most individual neural network operation types (activations, normalizations, element-wise operations) are memory bound, though the large matrix multiplications that dominate total compute time can be compute bound.

The key optimization for memory-bound operations is kernel fusion (also called operator fusion), combining multiple operations into a single GPU function (called a kernel)8 to avoid intermediate memory traffic. Instead of three round-trips to HBM, a fused kernel loads inputs from HBM once, evaluates normalization, dropout, and ReLU entirely within on-chip registers and SRAM, and writes only the final output tensor back to HBM, which cuts memory traffic by roughly \(3\times\). Attention kernels9 use the same principle at larger scale: instead of materializing the full attention matrix in HBM, a fused implementation can keep tiles close to the compute units, cut HBM accesses by up to 9\(\times\), and produce workload-specific speedups from 15 percent to 3\(\times\) (Dao et al. 2022).

8 Kernel (GPU): In GPU programming, a kernel is the function dispatched to execute in parallel across thousands of threads. Each kernel launch costs about 5 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s of CPU-side overhead for parameter assembly and GPU signaling, and an eager framework’s Python dispatch adds about 10 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s more before the launch, the split this chapter’s overhead model uses. Small, unfused operations therefore spend more time on launch overhead \((\Latency)\) than on useful arithmetic. Reducing kernel count through fusion is therefore a direct attack on the overhead term of the iron law.

9 Fused attention kernel: A fused attention kernel combines the \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) product, softmax, and value-weighted output into a tiled implementation that keeps intermediate values in on-chip memory rather than materializing the full attention matrix in HBM. FlashAttention is the canonical named example introduced in Memory requirements and reports up to 9\(\times\) fewer HBM accesses with workload-specific speedups from 15 percent to 3\(\times\) (Dao et al. 2022). The framework lesson is broader than the specific algorithm: fusion can shift an operation’s position on the Roofline Model from bandwidth-limited toward throughput-limited execution by reducing round-trips through external memory.

Dao, T., D. Y. Fu, S. Ermon, A. Rudra, and C. Ré. 2022. “FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 16344–59. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1189.

Framework compilers can fuse only operations that are visible to them simultaneously. When an eager runtime dispatches operations individually, each kernel launch executes in isolation, concealing cross-operation fusion and buffer-reuse opportunities. Capturing a broader region of computation into a deferred graph removes that isolation: the compiler can examine an entire sequence of dependencies, establish intermediate tensor lifetimes, and fuse compatible operations before dispatching work to the accelerator. How and when a framework captures that computation is therefore the core systems choice governing execution performance.

The computational graph

To expose multi-operation optimization opportunities to compilers, frameworks represent programs as a computational graph: a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where nodes represent operations and edges represent data dependencies. It is the same structure that backpropagation traverses in reverse, here serving as the framework’s internal model of the computation for scheduling and optimization as well.

Mathematical operations can be decoupled from physical execution through graph representations. Figure 2 diagrams this minimal abstraction: directed edges route input variables \(\FunctionInputVariable\) and \(\FunctionSecondInputVariable\) into the central transformation operator \(\IllustrativeFunction(\FunctionInputVariable, \FunctionSecondInputVariable)\), which emits the output variable \(\FunctionOutputVariable\) independent of physical evaluation order.

Real machine learning models require much more complex graph structures. Figure 3 partitions this system interaction into two coupled domains: the left container isolates a six-node neural network DAG with explicit dataflow edges, while the right container houses the runtime components—memory management and device placement—that interface with it. Across the central interface, the runtime queries tensor lifetimes to plan memory reuse and assigns operations to accelerator streams based on data dependencies rather than program order, optimizing execution before any arithmetic occurs.

This graph representation is more than a visualization; it is the data structure that enables both efficient execution and automatic differentiation. The timing of its construction forces a fundamental systems trade-off across debugging, optimization, deployment, and flexibility. Interactive debugging demands immediate execution so developers can inspect intermediate values and step through logic with standard tools. Global optimization requires observing large operator sequences at once to fuse kernels and plan memory reuse. Standalone deployment favors graphs that run without the host interpreter, while dynamic architectures require control flow that branches on computed tensor values. No single execution model serves all four demands at once.

Three execution strategies

The computational graph enables global optimization, but it leaves a critical design choice unresolved: when the runtime constructs the graph. Consider an elementary expression such as y = x * 2. Evaluating the multiplication immediately yields a concrete tensor in y. This imperative flow is intuitive and directly debuggable, but the runtime sees only one operation at a time. Deferring execution records the operation symbolically into a dependency graph executed only upon request. That staging separates intent from evaluation, giving the compiler the end-to-end visibility required for kernel fusion and memory planning.

Modern frameworks resolve this tension between flexibility and optimization potential with three execution strategies: eager execution with dynamic graphs, static computation graphs, and hybrid approaches that combine just-in-time (JIT) compilation with eager development.

Eager execution with dynamic graphs

Eager execution evaluates each operation immediately as the program calls it, building the computation graph dynamically at runtime. A side-by-side comparison shows how this differs from graph-based execution at the code level.

Example 7.1: Eager vs. graph execution code comparison
PyTorch (eager execution):

import torch

x = torch.tensor([1.0, 2.0])
y = x * 2
print(f"Intermediate value: {y}")  # Works immediately
z = y.sum()

TensorFlow 1.x (static graph):

import tensorflow as tf

x = tf.placeholder(tf.float32)
y = x * 2
# print(y) -> Prints Tensor("mul:0"...), not value!
z = tf.reduce_sum(y)

with tf.Session() as sess:
    result = sess.run(z, feed_dict={x: [1.0, 2.0]})

Systems insight: Eager execution exposes intermediate values as ordinary runtime state, which makes debugging direct. Static graphs stage computation before execution, which enables whole-graph optimization but changes the debugging model.

In this define-by-run model, when host code evaluates y = x * 2, the runtime dispatches the multiplication kernel to the device command queue and returns a tensor handle for subsequent operations. Device execution can continue asynchronously; dependent operations must wait for the result, and reading its value on the host can require synchronization.

This provides the flexibility of normal programming: developers can print intermediate values, use conditionals based on computed results, and debug with standard tools. The framework records operations as they happen, constructing a dynamic graph that reflects the actual execution path taken.

For gradient computation, the framework records a history of operations in what is called an autograd tape,10 a transient data structure built during execution. Each tensor operation creates a node that records: the operation performed, references to input tensors, and how to compute gradients. These nodes form a DAG that records the actual path taken during forward-pass execution rather than a graph fixed in advance. Listing 1 shows how PyTorch records operations as they execute in its default eager mode.

10 Autograd tape: A transient data structure built during forward execution, where nodes record operations, dependencies, and backward functions for chain-rule evaluation. Its memory footprint grows with model depth and sequence length as saved activations accumulate until released during backpropagation. For deep models, frameworks retain selected activations and recompute others via activation checkpointing to prevent OOM failures.

After these two operations, the framework has constructed an autograd tape with two nodes: one for the multiplication and one for the addition. The tape records that z depends on y, and y depends on x.

Calling z.backward() traverses this tape in reverse topological order, applying the chain rule at each node:

  1. Compute \(\frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable}{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable} = 1\) (seed gradient)
  2. Call AddBackward0.backward() \(\rightarrow \frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable}{\partial \TapeIntermediateResult} = 1\)
  3. Call MulBackward0.backward() \(\rightarrow \frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVariable}{\partial \FunctionInputVariable} = 2\)
  4. Accumulate gradient in x.grad

After backward() completes, autograd normally releases the saved state needed by its backward rules. Other live references can still retain tensor storage and graph nodes. The next forward pass builds a new graph. Values needed for gradients are saved during the forward pass and remain available until backward consumes them, so their memory cost spans both phases rather than appearing only during backward.

Example 7.2: In-place operations can break gradients
Scenario: An engineer applies an in-place mutation (x += 1) within a custom PyTorch activation function to reduce memory allocations.

Diagnosis: In-place operations overwrite tensor memory containing forward-pass activations required by the autograd tape for backward-pass gradient computation, triggering a runtime version-counter error (PyTorch Contributors 2026a). PyTorch enforces this safety invariant by maintaining an integer version counter on each tensor that increments on every in-place write; if a saved tensor’s version at backward execution differs from its version recorded during the forward pass, autograd throws an error because the activation required by the derivative rule was modified in place.

Systems lesson: Framework automatic differentiation depends on immutable forward-pass activation records. In-place writes to a saved tensor invalidate the autograd record, so PyTorch stops with an error rather than compute a wrong gradient.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026a. PyTorch Autograd Mechanics.

In eager execution frameworks, operations evaluate imperatively as host code encounters them. The define-by-run cycle is therefore a loop of one operation at a time (figure 4). Python defines an operation, the runtime enqueues its device work, and control returns to Python for the next call while the device may execute asynchronously. When the runtime decides what to launch, it sees the current operation rather than a whole region of future calls.

Systems implications: Flexibility

The dynamic autograd tape expresses data-dependent behavior directly in the host language. Conditionals and loops can depend on tensor values computed during execution, enabling algorithms such as beam search, dynamic-length recurrent models, or adaptive compute paths that adjust their execution based on intermediate activations. Static graphs can also represent data-dependent control flow and dynamic dimensions through graph operations, but eager execution makes these patterns natural to write and debug in Python. Because operations are issued immediately, developers can print tensors, inspect values, and use standard debuggers (pdb, breakpoints) to diagnose errors much as they would in another Python program.

Systems implications: Overhead

This flexibility imposes latency costs that map directly to the iron law. Each training step constructs an autograd tape dynamically, adding host-side Python dispatch latency and tape allocation to \(\Latency\). Because every operation traverses the framework dispatcher before a device kernel launches, host overhead quickly dominates when individual kernels execute in single-digit microseconds. Eager execution also lacks a whole-graph view, preventing the framework from fusing element-wise chains into single GPU launches or pre-allocating a static memory arena for intermediate activations (\(\DataVolume\)). The autograd tape must retain every tensor requested by registered backward rules, holding memory until backpropagation consumes it. Together, these costs impose a throughput ceiling on fine-grained workloads where dispatch latency overshadows device arithmetic.

Eager execution’s performance ceiling is driven by a fundamental systems mismatch: the speed of the host-side interpreter vs. the speed of the device-side silicon. The dispatch tax, defined here as the share of step time spent in host-side dispatch, \(\HostSoftwareTime/(\HostSoftwareTime+\DeviceHardwareTime)\) for host software time \(\HostSoftwareTime\) and device hardware time \(\DeviceHardwareTime\), quantifies this mismatch in an additive model without host-device overlap. Asynchronous execution can hide part of that host cost.

Fixed dispatch dominates small ops and fades behind large ones.

In the illustrative eager-execution model used here, every operation pays a fixed “tax” of approximately 15 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s for Python-side dispatch and type checks; this first comparison excludes the additional driver launch cost. Actual overhead varies by framework, backend, and hardware, but its relative weight still depends on operation size. For a small operation such as a ReLU on a small vector, the kernel might execute in only 1 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s, so the modeled dispatch tax reaches 94 percent and the GPU spends most of its time waiting for the next command. For a large operation such as a large matrix multiply, the kernel executes for 100 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s, the modeled dispatch tax drops to 13 percent, and host dispatch becomes a small share of the modeled time. The operation is device-work dominated; distinguishing compute from memory limits still requires comparing its work and traffic.

The dispatch tax explains why models with many small layers run significantly slower than their raw FLOP count predicts. Bottleneck diagnostic places this symptom in the bottleneck taxonomy, classifying a dispatch-dominated workload as latency-bound rather than compute-bound and showing which optimizations actually move it. Frameworks can move from kernel-by-kernel dispatch to graph-level execution, reducing repeated interpreter work and exposing operations that can be fused. A captured graph can still launch multiple kernels; fusion and runtime replay determine how much launch overhead remains. The hybrid JIT and compilation strategies in section 1.3.3.3 exist precisely to address this overhead.

The overhead costs of eager execution motivate the opposite design: capturing the entire computation before executing any of it. This is precisely what static computation graphs provide.

Static computation graphs

Static graph execution defines the complete computational graph as a symbolic representation first, then executes it separately. This “define-then-run” execution model means the graph exists before any computation occurs, enabling aggressive ahead-of-time optimization. Seeing the complete computation before launching device kernels allows the framework to analyze, transform, and optimize the graph globally—visibility unavailable across separate eager calls unless a compiler captures them. Operator fusion works through the canonical global transformation this enables: for \(\TensorElementCount\) elements of \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes each, fusing a chain of \(\ChainedOperationCount\) elementwise operations into one kernel reduces idealized read-and-write traffic from \(2\ChainedOperationCount \TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) to \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.

Two-phase execution

Static graphs implement a clear separation between graph construction and execution. Listing 2 illustrates the two phases using TensorFlow 1.x, which exemplified this approach. It deliberately runs the same x * 2 then + 1 computation shown under eager execution in listing 1, holding the arithmetic fixed so that the only thing that changes is when it executes: symbolic definition creates placeholders and operations without computation, while explicit execution triggers actual arithmetic.

Static graph compilation separates graph construction from tensor execution. In the definition phase, the program declares operations and variables and builds the symbolic DAG without allocating any tensor memory. Only in the execution phase do data load, concrete buffers materialize on the accelerator, and the captured graph run (figure 5).

The key difference from eager execution is that during construction, x, y, and z are not tensors containing values but rather symbolic nodes in a graph. Operators such as * and + append transformation nodes to the graph definition without issuing arithmetic. Calling print(y) makes this distinction visible: it prints symbolic tensor metadata rather than numerical values. Only when the host invokes sess.run() does the runtime analyze, optimize, and execute the captured graph on device buffers.

Ahead-of-time optimization

Because the framework has the complete graph before execution, it can perform ahead-of-time optimization unavailable across uncaptured eager calls. The kernel fusion opportunity introduced in section 1.3.1 becomes actionable here: because the framework sees y = x * 2 and z = y + 1 together in the graph, it can fuse them into z = x * 2 + 1, eliminating the intermediate y and halving idealized memory traffic. When tensor shapes and lifetimes are known, the compiler can plan memory before execution and reuse buffers whose lifetimes do not overlap; dynamic dimensions may require bounds or runtime allocation. Tensor layouts can also be transformed globally (for example, NCHW to NHWC) to match hardware preferences, though physical layout conversions may still require copies. Dead-code elimination (DCE)11 removes operations whose results are unused, and constant folding precomputes operations on constant values when their inputs are known. These optimizations map directly to iron law terms: kernel fusion can reduce \(\DataVolume\) by avoiding intermediate memory writes, constant folding reduces repeated work, planned allocation can reduce runtime overhead, and dead code elimination removes work and associated data movement when unused computations are present. Compilers such as XLA12 (Google 2025) extend this approach, compiling captured subgraphs into fused machine code specialized for target hardware.

11 Dead code elimination (DCE): Removes graph nodes whose results do not affect observable outputs or side effects. In ML graphs, dead code arises from unused branches or post-transform artifacts; side-effect analysis ensures safe removal to prevent execution of dead operations and reduce kernel launch overhead.

12 XLA (accelerated linear algebra): Compiling to fused machine code means XLA can fuse subgraphs, specialize layouts, and lower high-level operations into backend-specific code; fusion attacks both launch overhead \((\Latency)\) and intermediate memory writes \((\DataVolume)\), but the realized speedup depends on whether the graph contains enough fusible, memory-bound work for the compiler to remove. Large GEMM-heavy regions may already be compute bound, while chains of small elementwise operations can benefit more because fusion removes repeated trips through external memory. The benefit is not a fixed multiplier: XLA helps when it can fuse operations, specialize layouts and shapes, and reduce launch or memory overhead, so gains depend on graph structure, backend support, and input-shape stability.

Systems implications

Static graphs can achieve high performance through ahead-of-time optimization. Kernel fusion reduces memory bandwidth requirements when intermediate traffic is the bottleneck, and hardware-specific compilation can approach high utilization when shapes, operators, and layouts match efficient backend paths.

The cost of this performance is reduced flexibility. Standard Python control flow (if, for) cannot depend on computed tensor values in static graphs. TensorFlow provides graph-level control flow primitives (tf.cond and tf.while_loop) that support data-dependent conditions, but these require special syntax that diverges from standard Python, making code harder to write and reason about. Debugging is difficult because stack traces point to graph construction code, not execution code. Error messages often reference symbolic node names rather than the actual operations that failed.

Hybrid approaches: JIT compilation

JIT compilation pursues both eager debugging and graph optimization at once by capturing computation at runtime. The core trade-off is fidelity vs. generality. Tracing captures the execution path taken during a sample run, producing high fidelity to that path but missing branches not taken. Source-level compilation (scripting) analyzes supported program structure, preserving supported control flow but requiring a restricted language subset. Both approaches produce an intermediate representation (IR)13 that enables graph optimizations such as kernel fusion, constant folding, dead code elimination, and buffer reuse.

13 Intermediate representation (IR): The “intermediate” captures this format’s architectural role: a language-independent layer that decouples the frontend (Python capture) from the backend (hardware code generation), exactly as LLVM IR decouples C/Rust/Swift frontends from x86/ARM backends. ML frameworks adopted this compiler pattern because it reduces the \(\mathcal{O}(\CompilerFrontendCount\times \CompilerBackendCount)\) cost of supporting \(\CompilerFrontendCount\) frontends and \(\CompilerBackendCount\) backends to \(\mathcal{O}(\CompilerFrontendCount+ \CompilerBackendCount)\): a single graph capture mechanism (TorchDynamo, tf2xla) can target multiple hardware backends without rewriting the capture logic.

The eager-vs.-compiled trade-off has a direct iron law consequence. JIT compilation amortizes the \(\Latency\) (dispatch overhead) across the compiled region. Longer compiled regions mean more overhead amortized per operation, which explains why graph breaks are performance-critical: each break forces a return to eager dispatch, resetting the amortization.

Historically, PyTorch’s TorchScript exemplified both strategies (PyTorch Contributors 2026c). TorchScript is now deprecated in favor of capture and export paths such as torch.export (PyTorch Contributors 2026c, 2026b), but its two modes still make the fidelity-generality trade-off concrete. Tracing executed a function with example inputs and recorded the tensor operations observed on that path. Scripting instead compiled a restricted, type-annotated subset of Python source, preserving supported branches in the graph at the cost of rejecting unsupported Python objects, library calls, and metaprogramming. Both produced an IR with static types in static single-assignment (SSA) form, where each variable is assigned exactly once, so fusion, constant folding, and dead code elimination could run without the Python interpreter.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026c. TorchScript.
PyTorch Contributors. 2026b. torch.export.

The critical limitation of tracing is that a trace records operations from an observed path rather than preserving arbitrary Python control flow. Listing 3 illustrates the resulting correctness risk.

Tracing records the branch executed by the example input. In this example, converting the tensor condition to a Python Boolean commonly produces a TracerWarning; the resulting trace can still follow the captured branch for later inputs that require the other branch. Treating trace warnings as correctness failures is therefore essential when data-dependent Python control flow is present. The same hazard survives in modern graph-capture systems, which is why they guard each captured region with explicit assumptions about shapes and control flow.

Modern compilation: Graph-capture JIT

The previous approaches force a choice between flexible eager execution and compiler-visible graphs. Modern JIT compilation narrows this trade-off by automatically capturing regions of eager code into optimized graphs with limited developer intervention.

Graph-capture JIT systems follow the same architectural pattern across frameworks. The first execution observes tensor operations, records a graph region guarded by assumptions about shapes, dtypes, layouts, and control flow, lowers that region into an intermediate representation, applies fusion and layout optimizations, and caches executable code for later calls that satisfy the same guards. The compiled artifact is valid only while those assumptions hold, so a failed guard forces the runtime to recompile or fall back to eager execution. When eager fallback is enabled, unsupported Python code forms a graph boundary where execution returns to the eager runtime; a configuration requiring full-graph capture can instead report an error. PyTorch 2.0’s torch.compile (Ansel et al. 2024) is a concrete instance of this pattern, but the systems idea is broader than the API: compilation pays only when captured regions are long enough and stable enough to amortize capture, lowering, code generation, and cache-management costs.

Ansel, Jason, Edward Yang, Horace He, Natalia Gimelshein, Animesh Jain, Michael Voznesensky, Bin Bao, et al. 2024. “PyTorch 2: Faster Machine Learning Through Dynamic Python Bytecode Transformation and Graph Compilation.” Proceedings of the 29th ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems, Volume 2, 929–47. https://doi.org/10.1145/3620665.3640366.

Compilation therefore attacks the dispatch tax of section 1.3.3.1 directly, and fusion adds a second saving in memory traffic. The physics of software overhead in notebook 7.1 models both.

Napkin Math 7.1: The physics of software overhead
Iron law connection: The latency term \((\Latency)\) in the iron law is dominated by software overhead: dispatching instructions from Python to the GPU. Each operation pays a Python dispatch cost of ~10 μs plus a kernel launch cost of ~5 μs, which together set the per-operation overhead applied in the two scenarios below. Python dispatch covers the framework call; driver launch covers submission to the device. A compiled region invoked from Python still pays an entry cost, whereas replay of a captured launch sequence removes repeated per-operation Python work and has its own submission cost.

Scenario one: Eager Mode (The “Tiny Op” Trap) Consider a simple activation block, y = relu(x + bias), in which the input and output each contain \(\TensorElementCount\) elements of \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes each. Assume a scalar or broadcast bias remains in fast memory, so the traffic model counts the full input, intermediate, and output tensors.

  • Operations: Two (Add, ReLU).
  • Execution:
    1. Launch Add Kernel: 15 μs overhead.
    2. Read/Write Memory: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.
    3. Launch ReLU Kernel: 15 μs overhead.
    4. Read/Write Memory: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.
  • Total overhead: 30 μs.
  • Total memory traffic: \(4\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes.

Scenario two: Compiled Mode (Fusion) The compiler fuses this into one kernel: FusedAddRelu. This scenario retains one ordinary Python entry and one driver launch, applying the same combined cost as eager mode to isolate the effect of reducing two calls to one. It does not model captured-graph replay.

  • Execution:
    1. Launch Fused Kernel: 15 μs overhead.
    2. Read/Write Memory: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes (the intermediate result stays on chip).
  • Total overhead: 15 μs (2× speedup).
  • Total memory traffic: \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes (2× bandwidth efficiency).

Systems insight: Fusion wins on two fronts at once. Collapsing two launches into one halves the per-op dispatch overhead in this scenario, and keeping the intermediate result on chip cuts idealized memory traffic from \(4\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) to \(2\TensorElementCount\ElementStorageSize\) bytes. For small element-wise operations, the avoided round-trip to external memory can matter more than the arithmetic.

Scaling this arithmetic across a sequence of five small operations demonstrates how host dispatch latency starves the accelerator (figure 6). In eager execution, each 1 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s kernel incurs 10 μs of Python dispatch overhead, leaving the accelerator idle for over 90 percent of the sequence. Compiling the sequence resolves both constraints simultaneously: the framework submits the work in a single launch of 5 μs, eliminating all intermediate host dispatch gaps, while operator fusion keeps intermediate activations in registers or cache to complete the entire sequence in 4 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s instead of the uncompiled 5 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\)s.

Graph-capture compilers such as torch.compile14 automate this fusion over captured regions without requiring engineers to write custom CUDA.15 However, compilation cannot span arbitrary Python code: graph breaks mark the boundary where compilation ceases to apply. Data-dependent Python control flow, unsupported library calls, I/O, custom Python objects, and highly variable shapes can shorten compiled regions. With eager fallback enabled, a break separates compiled subgraphs with standard Python execution, reintroducing interpreter dispatch and limiting fusion across the boundary. Tensor handles can pass between these regions while their storage remains on the device; host reads of device values can additionally require synchronization. This is why graph-break analysis belongs in performance engineering: the relevant metric is not whether compilation is enabled, but how much of the hot path remains inside long, stable compiled regions.

14 torch.compile: PyTorch’s capture front end analyzes Python bytecode as each frame runs, rather than recording tensor operations alone. That vantage lets it keep capturing across Python control flow it can resolve from guarded values and insert a graph break where it cannot, which is why graph breaks rather than tracing errors are its characteristic failure mode.

15 CUDA (compute unified device architecture): NVIDIA’s parallel computing platform (2007) serving as the foundational layer between high-level Python operations and GPU silicon; when PyTorch executes torch.matmul(A, W), the call traverses the framework’s dispatcher, selects a cuBLAS kernel, and launches it on the GPU. Each launch adds the 5 μs kernel-launch share of the per-operation tax on top of the framework’s Python dispatch. For small operations, this dispatch overhead \((\Latency)\) can exceed useful compute time. Fusing compatible groups among \(\DispatchedOperationCount\) operations then reduces launch count as well as intermediate memory traffic; capturing the graph alone does not imply a single kernel.

Backends occupy different points on the flexibility-performance spectrum. A JIT compiler traces model execution at runtime to generate optimized kernels dynamically, incurring initial warmup latency during the first pass. An ahead-of-time (AOT) compiler instead compiles captured computation before deployment, shifting compilation out of the hot path and enabling a standalone runtime when the export target supports one. A general JIT backend optimizes ordinary training and serving workloads with moderate compilation cost; a specialized inference backend can apply deeper fusion, precision lowering, and autotuning when the deployment target is fixed; an ahead-of-time mobile or embedded runtime removes even more flexibility to gain footprint and predictability. The same rule governs all of them: the narrower the target and the more stable the graph, the more optimization the compiler can safely perform.

The resulting workflow is a systems decision. Rapid prototyping favors eager execution because architecture changes and guard failures make recompilation cost visible. Long training runs and high-volume inference amortize compilation cost over many executions, provided the model has stable shapes and limited graph breaks. Debugging usually starts in eager mode because errors map directly to source code; compilation is reintroduced after the model behavior is correct and the performance bottleneck is measurable.

Comparison of execution models

Table 1 contrasts the three execution models across six dimensions, showing how hybrid JIT compilation can recover graph-level optimization within captured regions while preserving eager fallback outside them.

Eager mode’s primary value is in the iteration loop: it allows using standard Python debuggers (such as pdb) to inspect variables mid-execution, whereas graph-mode debugging often requires specialized framework tools. This immediate feedback accelerates the prototyping phase of the ML lifecycle.

↩︎ The iteration tax, the cost that slow experiment cycles impose on how many candidate models a team can test, is established in Quantifying the ML Lifecycle.

Beyond these core execution trade-offs, table 2 highlights additional systems-level distinctions between static and dynamic approaches.

These trade-offs are not binary choices. Modern frameworks offer a spectrum of execution options, which raises the quantitative question of where on this spectrum a given project should operate.

Quantitative principles of execution

These execution models form a spectrum, and two quantitative principles make the choice measurable in practice. The compilation continuum principle asks when compilation gains justify development cost by comparing production executions with development iterations. The dispatch overhead law shows why eager execution can spend more time dispatching small operations than computing them.

The compilation continuum principle

The execution problem demands a quantitative principle for when a project should compile. The execution models form a continuum from maximum flexibility to maximum optimization. Equation 1 lays out the four positions on that axis, and each labeled arrow names the mechanism that carries a project one step rightward toward hardware.

\[ \text{Eager} \xrightarrow{\text{capture}} \text{Graph JIT} \xrightarrow{\text{export}} \text{AOT Runtime} \xrightarrow{\text{specialize}} \text{Hardware} \tag{1}\]

Each step rightward sacrifices flexibility for performance. The practical question is where a given project should be placed on this continuum. The optimal compilation strategy depends on production executions, development recompilations, and their respective time costs, combined in equation 2:

\[ \text{Compilation Benefit} = \frac{\ProductionExecutionCount\cdot (\EagerExecutionTime- \CompiledExecutionTime)}{\CompilationTime+ \DevelopmentIterationCount\cdot \CompilationTime} \tag{2}\]

where:

  • \(\ProductionExecutionCount\) = number of production executions (dimensionless count: inference requests, training steps)
  • \(\DevelopmentIterationCount\) = number of development iterations requiring recompilation (dimensionless count)
  • \(\EagerExecutionTime\) = time per execution in eager mode (seconds)
  • \(\CompiledExecutionTime\) = time per execution in compiled mode (seconds)
  • \(\CompilationTime\) = time per compilation or recompilation (seconds; assumed constant)

This model assumes that the compiled artifact remains valid between development changes and that the initial compilation and each recompilation have the same cost; under these assumptions, the decision rule is to compile when \(\text{Compilation Benefit} > 1\). The ratio is dimensionless.

Table 3 prices that decision rule with hypothetical throughputs and compilation times across execution modes and model architectures:

These throughput differences are not uniform across workloads, so the execution strategy must align with the operational regime of each workload archetype. The appropriate strategy depends on the computational phase, the target backend, and the dominant iron law term. In batched prefill, transformers exhibit high operational intensity that benefits from dense matrix compilation; in small-batch autoregressive decoding, however, the workload shifts into a bandwidth-bound regime where memory transfers dominate. Table 4 maps each recurring archetype to a candidate execution strategy based on these primary physical constraints.

↩︎ The workload archetypes and the iron law term that dominates each are established in Workload archetypes.

Lighthouse 7.1: Framework strategy by archetype
The four recurring archetypes in table 4 trace one principle across the framework: compilation benefits scale with how much of the workload is optimizable.

Systems insight: In the illustrative scenario, Compute Beasts such as the ResNet-50 row in table 3 benefit because their dense kernels expose substantial surface for layout selection, precision lowering, and fusion. Bandwidth Hog workloads such as small-batch GPT-2 generation require distinguishing intermediate activation traffic from parameter streaming: fusing attention kernels eliminates round-trips for transient activation tensors and removes dispatch latency, but memory bandwidth continues to bind on streaming model weights and key-value caches unless batching increases operational intensity. Sparse Scatter workloads such as DLRM can gain less when irregular embedding lookups leave limited compiler scope.

Three cases illustrate how reuse changes the compilation decision. In research prototyping (\(\DevelopmentIterationCount\gg \ProductionExecutionCount\)), teams should stay eager. If the architecture changes every few minutes, compilation overhead can dominate. Under the scenario’s 30-second compilation assumption, ten compilations per hour consume five minutes.

For repeated execution (\(\ProductionExecutionCount\gg \DevelopmentIterationCount\)), compilation can pay off because its one-time cost is amortized across many calls. In the hypothetical ResNet-50 inference scenario in table 3, torch.compile provides 48.3 percent higher throughput (2,150 img/s vs. 1,450 img/s). Using the scenario’s 30 s compile cost, this pays off after the breakeven point in equation 3:

\[ \CompilationBreakevenCount= \frac{\CompilationTime}{\EagerExecutionTime- \CompiledExecutionTime} \tag{3}\]

Evaluating equation 3 with the hypothetical ResNet-50 values gives approximately 134,000 images. The same amortization principle applies to long training runs, using training throughput and compilation costs measured for that workload.

Long-lived production inference (\(\DevelopmentIterationCount\approx 0\), \(\ProductionExecutionCount\rightarrow \infty\)) extends this reuse further, providing more opportunity to amortize compilation. Aggressive autotuning can be worthwhile even when setup takes much longer, provided the model converts correctly, meets numerical requirements, and improves measured performance over the deployment lifetime.

The cases form a continuum of increasing reuse (figure 7). Each strategy’s total time starts at its one-time compilation cost and grows at its per-execution cost, so eager wins at low execution counts, JIT wins once its compilation is amortized, and AOT wins only at the largest counts, where its higher upfront cost is fully repaid. The figure’s crossovers (near \(2 \times 10^6\) and \(6.7 \times 10^6\) executions) come from an illustrative cost model in arbitrary units, not from the ResNet-50 scenario, whose eager-to-compiled breakeven sits near 134,000 images. What carries over is the order of the regions, not their positions.

The dispatch overhead law

A second principle, the dispatch overhead law, emerges from equation 4, which identifies the regime in which framework overhead, rather than compute or memory, dominates execution time. Let \(\DispatchedOperationCount\) be the number of operations (count), \(\OperationDispatchOverhead\) the per-operation dispatch overhead (seconds), and \(\IronLawComputeTime\) and \(\IronLawMemoryTime\) the total compute and memory times (seconds). The ratio uses the additive, no-overlap approximation \(\DeviceHardwareTime\approx \IronLawComputeTime + \IronLawMemoryTime\); when execution overlaps those costs, measured hardware time should replace their sum. Framework overhead dominates when operations are small relative to dispatch cost:

\[ \text{Overhead Ratio} = \frac{\DispatchedOperationCount\cdot \OperationDispatchOverhead}{\IronLawComputeTime + \IronLawMemoryTime} \tag{4}\]

When Overhead Ratio \(> 1\), the model is overhead-bound. The ratio and the dispatch tax introduced with eager execution measure the same imbalance from two sides. With \(\HostSoftwareTime= \DispatchedOperationCount\cdot \OperationDispatchOverhead\), the tax share \(\HostSoftwareTime/(\HostSoftwareTime+\DeviceHardwareTime)\) equals \(\text{Overhead Ratio}/(1+\text{Overhead Ratio})\), so a ratio above 1 is the same threshold as a dispatch tax above 50 percent. Compilation can provide its largest gains for overhead-bound workloads because fusion reduces the number of dispatches. The training-step trace in section 1.10 works this effect through end to end, previewed by the numbers that follow.

Applying equation 4 means comparing the software overhead \(\HostSoftwareTime= \DispatchedOperationCount\cdot \OperationDispatchOverhead\) with the hardware execution time \(\DeviceHardwareTime\) (both in seconds). The regime depends on the size of individual operations rather than on the model’s parameter count. Notebook 7.2 contrasts a small multilayer perceptron with a large transformer layer to quantify when host overhead or device arithmetic bounds the system.

Napkin Math 7.2: Dispatch overhead at two scales
Problem: Determine the threshold where host-side Python interpreter dispatch overhead bounds end-to-end execution time relative to accelerator hardware execution.

Scenario one: Small multilayer perceptron (MLP) (Overhead Bound)

  • Compute: 6 ops across small matrix/element-wise operations.
  • Hardware time: \(\DeviceHardwareTime\approx\) 2.6 μs (mostly memory latency).
  • Software overhead: \(\HostSoftwareTime\approx\) 6 ops \(\times\) 15 μs/op = 90 μs.
  • Ratio: 90 μs/2.6 μs ≈ 34.6.
  • Small-model outcome: The system spends 97 percent of time in host-side dispatch and kernel-launch overhead. Eliminating all modeled dispatch overhead would give an upper-bound speedup of 35.6×.

Scenario two: GPT-3 Layer (Compute Bound)

  • Compute: Huge matrix multiplications.
  • Assumptions: Both times below are illustrative round values for one GPT-3-scale layer over a large batch, not derived from a specific accelerator. The conclusion does not hinge on them, because at the chapter’s 15 μs/op the ratio would reach 1 only if the layer issued thousands of separately dispatched kernels.
  • Hardware time: \(\DeviceHardwareTime\approx\) 100 ms = 100000 μs.
  • Software overhead: \(\HostSoftwareTime \approx 50 \, \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\).
  • Ratio: 50 μs/100000 μs ≈ 0.0005.
  • Large-model outcome: Modeled Python overhead is negligible. Compilation may still improve fusion, layout, or backend specialization; dispatch elimination contributes little.

Systems insight: Dispatch overhead is regime-dependent. Compilation can reduce host-side overhead for small-operation workloads by fusing operations into fewer launches, while large models benefit mainly from fused kernels and memory movement reductions.

The dispatch tax analysis demonstrates that small operations become overhead-bound when dispatch latency exceeds device execution time. This systems constraint reaches its logical extreme at the edge of the deployment spectrum, where the memory and latency footprint of the Python runtime exceeds the physical capacity of the target hardware.

Frameworks for the edge: TinyML and micro-runtimes

The compilation continuum reaches its extreme at the far edge. While cloud frameworks such as PyTorch and TensorFlow 2.x prioritize flexibility through eager execution, TinyML16 systems operating on microcontrollers (MCUs) with kilobytes of memory cannot afford the overhead of a Python interpreter or a fully dynamic runtime.

16 TinyML: Systems designed for MCUs cannot afford a Python interpreter. Micro-runtimes instead use small C/C++ runtimes, fixed memory planning, and model-specific operators. TensorFlow Lite Micro remains interpreter-based: the application supplies a tensor arena whose buffers are planned and reused without heap allocation after setup. Predictable memory is mandatory because exceeding a 256 KB device budget can prevent initialization or inference.

Lighthouse 7.2: Lighthouse example: KWS on TinyML
Scenario: Deploying the keyword spotting (KWS) lighthouse, a DS-CNN and the volume’s Tiny Constraint workload, to an ARM Cortex-M4 microcontroller with 256 KB RAM and 1 MB Flash. This is a smaller target than the ESP32-S3 of the earlier smart-doorbell scenario, chosen to show the runtime at its tightest.

Constraint: A full PyTorch and Python runtime exceeds the device’s memory budget by orders of magnitude.

Framework solution: Micro-frameworks such as TensorFlow Lite Micro (TFLM) (David et al. 2021) solve this through a tiny interpreter-based runtime with a fixed memory discipline:

  1. Fixed memory arena: The application supplies a contiguous tensor arena, and the framework plans and reuses buffers from that arena rather than relying on dynamic allocation during inference.
  2. Kernel selection: Only the specific kernels used by the model (for example, Conv2D, DepthwiseConv) need to be linked or registered with the runtime.
  3. Compact interpreter execution: The MCU runs a small C/C++ interpreter over a flat model representation, with the model and arena bound at initialization rather than assembled dynamically at runtime.

Silicon contract: On TinyML devices, the binding term is memory capacity, not bandwidth or dispatch overhead, which is why table 4 treats it as a feasibility gate. The framework’s primary job is to ensure the model’s intermediate activations (the “working set”) fit within the MCU’s tiny SRAM.

David, Robert, Jared Duke, Advait Jain, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Nat Jeffries, Jian Li, Nick Kreeger, et al. 2021. “TensorFlow Lite Micro: Embedded Machine Learning for TinyML Systems.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 3: 800–811.

These micro-runtimes sacrifice dynamic flexibility to reduce footprint and make memory predictable. With compact models, low-power hardware, and duty cycling, that discipline enables milliwatt-scale inference with mostly on-chip data movement.

The spectrum of execution strategies, from dynamic eager execution to static graph compilation and specialized micro-runtimes, requires developers to make deliberate trade-offs. The key execution-mode decisions summarize these architectural choices:

Checkpoint 7.1: Execution models

The choice of execution mode determines both developer velocity and model performance.

Debuggability vs. speed

Modern compromise

The execution problem determines when computation happens and what optimizations are possible. Neural network training, however, requires a capability that no amount of clever scheduling can provide: the ability to compute gradients automatically.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A GPU performance profile reveals that a sequence of LayerNorm, dropout, and GELU activation operations spends over 80% of its execution time reading and writing intermediate tensors to High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) with very low arithmetic intensity. Why is operator fusion the primary framework optimization for this workload?

    1. It replaces 16-bit floating-point arithmetic with 8-bit integer arithmetic
    2. It changes the model architecture to eliminate all non-linear activation functions
    3. It converts compute-bound matrix multiplications into memory-bound operations
    4. It fuses multiple sequential element-wise operations into a single GPU kernel, keeping intermediate values in on-chip SRAM/registers and eliminating redundant round trips to HBM

  2. A developer traces a PyTorch model containing data-dependent control flow such as if tensor.sum() > 0: ... using torch.jit.trace, then later runs inputs that should take the other branch. What is the most accurate description of the tracing risk and the modern graph-capture behavior described in the section?

    1. Tracing always crashes immediately on tensor-dependent branches, whereas modern graph capture always compiles the entire program without fallback
    2. Tracing converts the Python conditional into a fully general static graph branch, whereas modern graph capture removes the need for any control-flow assumptions
    3. Tracing records the branch taken by the example input and may emit a TracerWarning, while modern graph capture can compile supported regions and, when eager fallback is enabled, return to eager execution at unsupported boundaries
    4. Tracing silently succeeds for all future inputs, whereas modern graph capture fails only when the GPU lacks enough memory

  3. State the dispatch overhead law and explain why a workload composed of many small tensor operations on a fast GPU can be severely underutilized in eager mode even when the hardware has high peak throughput.

  4. True or False: When eager fallback is enabled in torch.compile, encountering a graph break necessarily halts execution with a runtime error because the unsupported Python code cannot be represented in the captured graph.

  5. Order the stages of a generic graph-capture compilation pipeline from the first Python call to repeated execution of optimized code: (1) an intermediate representation is built for the captured region, (2) optimized executable code is cached for reuse under the same guards, (3) the frontend captures a region of tensor computation from the eager program, (4) the compiled region executes on the target hardware, (5) optimization and lowering transform the captured region toward hardware-specific execution.

  6. In graph-capture JIT compilation systems like TorchDynamo, an event where the compiler encounters an unsupported dynamic Python construct (such as an unhandled C-extension call or dynamic side effect) and must pause graph capture to yield control to the Python interpreter is called a ____.

Differentiation Problem

Training requires determining how an incremental change in each of millions of parameters alters the scalar loss. Doing this manually for even a simple three-layer network requires deriving and implementing dozens of partial derivatives; for a modern transformer with billions of parameters, manual differentiation is intractable. A framework that executes efficiently but cannot differentiate can run inference but cannot learn.

The differentiation problem is the task of computing gradients17 automatically. Neural network training requires derivatives of a scalar loss \(\LossFunction\) with respect to millions or billions of parameters. When executing the forward pass, the runtime records an execution tape and saves intermediate activation buffers for subsequent derivative evaluations. Backpropagation already showed why reverse-mode automatic differentiation (AD)18 suits this shape of problem, since one reverse traversal from a single scalar loss yields every parameter gradient, a claim the forward-versus-reverse comparison below makes precise. Major ML frameworks therefore use reverse-mode AD for ordinary scalar-loss training (Baydin et al. 2018).

17 Gradients: A gradient holds one value for every parameter it differentiates, so each weight tensor acquires a gradient tensor of the same shape. Computing gradients automatically therefore doubles the parameter-sized state before any optimizer runs, which is why every training-memory ledger later in this chapter lists gradients as a full copy of the weights.

18 Reverse-mode AD: Reverse mode’s single backward pass has a concrete price, because the operation-dependent values that derivative rules read must be retained until the backward traversal reaches them. Activation checkpointing changes this saved set by omitting selected values and recomputing them during the backward pass.

Baydin, Atilim Gunes, Barak A. Pearlmutter, Alexey Andreyevich Radul, and Jeffrey Mark Siskind. 2018. “Automatic Differentiation in Machine Learning: A Survey.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 18 (153): 1–43.

Building on the backpropagation algorithm, the systems engineering of differentiation addresses how frameworks represent computation graphs, manage memory for intermediate values, and orchestrate the backward pass efficiently across accelerators. The framework mechanizes the chain rule as large-scale systems bookkeeping: tracking operator dependencies, staging activation buffers, and dispatching backward kernels. Listing 4 illustrates the core idea with a simple three-operation function.

↪ The optimization algorithms that consume these gradients are developed in Optimization algorithms.

Frameworks decompose this function into elementary operations, each with a known local derivative, and then combine these local derivatives via the chain rule to compute gradients through supported compositions. The systems challenge is executing this transformation under physical hardware constraints: the framework must represent the computation graph, make necessary forward values available, and schedule backward kernels without exhausting accelerator HBM. Production runtimes address these challenges through structured graph recording, caching allocators, and reverse-mode traversal.

Forward and reverse mode differentiation

Automatic differentiation operates in two distinct traversal directions: forward mode and reverse mode. The choice between them governs whether gradient evaluation cost scales with the number of input dimensions or output dimensions. Because deep learning objectives evaluate a single scalar loss with respect to millions or billions of parameters, this mathematical asymmetry dictates the memory footprint and execution schedule of the underlying framework.

Forward mode

Forward mode automatic differentiation computes directional derivatives concurrently with the primal evaluation. For each intermediate variable \(\TraceIntermediateVariable_i\), the runtime evaluates both the primal value \(\TraceIntermediateVariable_i\) and its directional derivative (tangent) \(\TraceIntermediateTangent_i = \sum_j \frac{\partial \TraceIntermediateVariable_i}{\partial \FunctionInputVariable_j} \FunctionInputTangent_j\). Because tangents flow downstream alongside primal values, forward mode requires no execution tape and never retains forward activations for a subsequent backward pass.

To see the mechanism concretely, consider computing both the value and derivative of \(\IllustrativeFunction(\FunctionInputVariable) = \FunctionInputVariable^2 \sin(\FunctionInputVariable)\). Listing 5 shows how forward mode evaluates local derivative rules alongside each elementary operation, applying the chain and product rules at every step:

Forward mode mechanizes this paired tracking through dual numbers—values of the form \(\TraceIntermediateVariable+ \TraceIntermediateTangent\DualNumberInfinitesimal\) where \(\DualNumberInfinitesimal^2 = 0\). Evaluating an arithmetic operator or elementary function over dual numbers automatically yields the updated primal and tangent without graph retention. Listing 6 demonstrates this trace evaluated at \(\FunctionInputVariable= 2.0\) with seed \(\FunctionInputTangent= 1.0\):

A single forward pass with a seeded tangent vector \(\TangentSeedVector\) evaluates a Jacobian-vector product (JVP), \(\FunctionJacobian\TangentSeedVector\), where \(\FunctionJacobian\in \mathbb{R}^{\FunctionOutputDimension\times \Parameters}\) is the Jacobian matrix. When an objective maps \(\Parameters\) parameter inputs to a single scalar loss (\(\FunctionOutputDimension= 1\)), recovering the full gradient \(\nabla_{\ModelParameters}\LossFunction \in \mathbb{R}^{\Parameters}\) requires setting \(\TangentSeedVector\) to each of the \(\Parameters\) standard basis vectors in turn. Recovering gradients with forward mode therefore requires \(\Parameters\) distinct forward evaluations (or propagating \(\Parameters\)-wide tangent bundles), scaling compute and memory traffic as \(\mathcal{O}(\Parameters)\) with parameter count. For networks with millions or billions of parameters, this scaling makes forward-mode gradient calculation intractable, although forward JVPs remain highly effective when input dimensions are small or for evaluating directional derivatives and Hessian-vector products.

Reverse mode

Reverse-mode automatic differentiation inverts the traversal order to exploit the many-to-one geometry of neural network training. Instead of propagating tangents downstream from inputs, reverse mode propagates adjoints \(\TraceIntermediateAdjoint_i = \partial \LossFunction / \partial \TraceIntermediateVariable_i\) upstream from the scalar loss \(\LossFunction\) to all parameters.

Mathematically, while forward mode computes Jacobian-vector products (\(\FunctionJacobian\TangentSeedVector\)), reverse mode evaluates vector-Jacobian products (VJPs), \(\UpstreamGradientVector^T \FunctionJacobian= \sum_{j=1}^\FunctionOutputDimension \UpstreamGradientElement_j \, \partial \FunctionOutputElement_j / \partial \FunctionInputVector\), where \(\UpstreamGradientElement_j = \partial \LossFunction / \partial \FunctionOutputElement_j\), one element of \(\UpstreamGradientVector\). A VJP multiplies an upstream gradient vector by the local operation’s Jacobian.

Evaluating VJPs avoids a prohibitive memory cost. Consider an intermediate layer mapping an input vector \(\FunctionInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\FunctionInputDimension\) to an output \(\FunctionOutputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\FunctionOutputDimension\), where \(\FunctionInputDimension= \FunctionOutputDimension= 100{,}000\). The full Jacobian \(\FunctionJacobian\in \mathbb{R}^{\FunctionOutputDimension\times \FunctionInputDimension}\) contains \(10^{10}\) elements, requiring \(40\text{ GB}\) of memory to store a single layer’s derivatives in FP32. Backpropagation, however, never needs this full matrix. Because training optimizes a single scalar loss \(\LossFunction\), the backward pass only ever needs to propagate an upstream gradient vector \(\UpstreamGradientVector= \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \FunctionOutputVector} \in \mathbb{R}^{\FunctionOutputDimension}\).

To see how VJPs sidestep the massive matrix, consider a simple element-wise operation like \(\FunctionOutputVector= \text{ReLU}(\FunctionInputVector)\):

  1. The inputs: An input vector \(\FunctionInputVector= [-1.0, 2.0, 3.0]\) and an upstream gradient \(\UpstreamGradientVector= [0.5, 0.5, 0.5]\) flowing backward from the loss.
  2. The local derivative: The derivative of ReLU is \(1\) if \(\FunctionInputVariable> 0\), else \(0\). The local gradient is \(\frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVector}{\partial \FunctionInputVector} = [0, 1, 1]\).
  3. The VJP: Instead of building a \(3{\times}3\) diagonal matrix and executing a matrix multiplication, the VJP computes an element-wise product: \(\UpstreamGradientVector\odot \frac{\partial \FunctionOutputVector}{\partial \FunctionInputVector} = [0.0, 0.5, 0.5]\).

By evaluating the product directly, the runtime streams vectors without ever materializing the massive \(\FunctionOutputDimension\times \FunctionInputDimension\) matrix in memory. Seeding the reverse sweep with \(\LossAdjointSeed = 1\) yields the complete parameter gradient vector \(\nabla_{\ModelParameters}\LossFunction\) in a single backward pass, regardless of whether the model contains ten parameters or ten billion.

Reverse mode operates on the same three-operation function \(\IllustrativeFunction(\FunctionInputVariable) = \FunctionInputVariable^2\sin(\FunctionInputVariable)\) from listing 4, where \(\FunctionInputVariable\) reaches the output through two distinct computational paths: the square and the sine. Algorithm 1 defines the reverse-mode traversal algorithm over a directed acyclic graph before tracing this concrete evaluation.

\(y\)

The algorithmic structure of algorithm 1 reveals its systems cost. The forward pass records operator dependencies and buffers intermediate values required by backward derivative rules until the reverse sweep consumes them. Reverse mode trades activation storage and graph bookkeeping for a single backward pass, whose cost is a small constant multiple of the forward pass regardless of parameter count.

For the concrete function in listing 4 evaluated at \(\FunctionInputVariable=2.0\), the forward pass records \(\WorkedTraceSquare=\FunctionInputVariable^2=4.0\), \(\WorkedTraceSine=\sin(\FunctionInputVariable)\approx0.9093\), and \(\WorkedTraceProduct=\WorkedTraceSquare \WorkedTraceSine\approx3.637\). Seeding \(\WorkedTraceProductAdjoint=1.0\), the product rule yields adjoints \(\WorkedTraceSquareAdjoint=0.9093\) and \(\WorkedTraceSineAdjoint=4.0\). The square branch contributes \(0.9093\cdot4.0\approx3.6372\) to \(\FunctionInputAdjoint\), while the sine branch contributes \(4.0\cos(2.0)\approx-1.6646\). Accumulating both paths gives the exact derivative \(\partial \WorkedTraceProduct/\partial \FunctionInputVariable= \FunctionInputAdjoint\approx 1.973\).

This single backward traversal computes \(\partial \WorkedTraceProduct/\partial \FunctionInputVariable\) regardless of how many fan-out paths connect \(\FunctionInputVariable\) to \(\WorkedTraceProduct\). In a deep neural network, every weight tensor influences the scalar loss through thousands of divergent paths across successive layers; reverse mode aggregates all incoming adjoints in one reverse sweep. That one sweep, rather than one pass per parameter, is what keeps training a 175B-parameter model such as GPT-3 computationally tractable.

Executing this traversal in hardware exposes a physical constraint: evaluating local derivatives during the backward pass requires the operating point established during forward execution. The framework must therefore determine which forward tensors to persist in device memory, when to allocate them, and when to free them. The recorded graph answers the “when to free” question, because each saved tensor can be released as soon as the last backward rule that reads it has run. The peak itself is bounded separately, by splitting a large batch into micro-batches whose saved activations are freed before the next one starts while their gradients sum into the same buffers (gradient accumulation).19

19 Gradient accumulation: The framework processes a large logical batch as smaller mini-batches, freeing each mini-batch’s saved activations after backward while accumulating parameter-sized gradients. Processing 4,096 samples in 64-sample mini-batches can reduce the batch-dependent activation peak by up to 64\(\times\) when activation memory scales linearly with batch size; weights, gradients, optimizer state, and other fixed costs do not shrink. The trade-off is more sequential work before each optimizer update.

Listing 7 illustrates these requirements in a two-layer network, showing the forward pass storing intermediate activations and the backward pass consuming them to evaluate parameter gradients.

Three implementation requirements emerge from this example. First, the framework must track dependencies between operations to determine the correct reverse traversal order. Second, the information needed by each backward rule must be available, either saved during the forward pass or regenerated. The chain rule evaluates local derivatives at the forward operating point—for example, evaluating \(\frac{\partial}{\partial \NeuronPreactivation}\text{ReLU}(\NeuronPreactivation) = \IndicatorFunction(\NeuronPreactivation> 0)\) requires the sign of the pre-activation \(\NeuronPreactivation\), which can be recovered from a saved value or mask, and computing a dense layer’s weight gradient \(\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \WeightMatrix} = \LayerInputMatrix^T \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \PreactivationMatrix}\), where \(\PreactivationMatrix\) is the layer’s pre-activation output, requires the forward input activation tensor \(\LayerInputMatrix\). Third, every differentiated operation needs both a forward implementation and a corresponding backward rule. These requirements define the engineering surface of automatic differentiation, where retaining forward values can consume much of the training memory budget.

Memory management strategies

A 175B-parameter model in FP16 requires 350 GB just for weights, exceeding the physical memory of any single accelerator. Reverse-mode AD compounds this memory pressure by retaining operation-dependent forward activations for backward derivative rules. Even a model whose weights fit easily pays this tax. In the illustrative ResNet-50 scenario of section 1.4.2.1.1, batch-64 saved activations exceed the FP32 weights by roughly two orders of magnitude, on top of weights, gradients, and optimizer states. Memory capacity, rather than raw floating-point throughput, frequently establishes the feasibility boundary of training.

This saved-activation footprint accumulates across successive layers. Listing 8 demonstrates how each forward layer adds an activation tensor that must persist until the backward sweep unwinds to that layer.

This is a capacity limit rather than the bandwidth gap of the memory wall, and frameworks relieve it through two primary architectural strategies. The first is activation checkpointing (or gradient checkpointing): rather than buffering every intermediate activation, the runtime stores only selected segment boundary tensors and recomputes dropped intermediates on demand during the backward pass. The essential systems mechanism is the runtime contract: the framework marks boundary activations as persistent and regenerates intermediate values when backward execution reaches that segment. Listing 9 illustrates this contract: the forward pass preserves only segment boundaries, and the backward pass re-executes the forward segment to regenerate activations rather than loading them from global memory.

↪ Activation checkpointing schedules and their recomputation cost are operationalized in Optimal checkpoint placement strategy.

The second strategy is the kernel fusion that the memory wall motivated in section 1.3.1, applied here to the body of a layer. Its capacity benefit is narrower than its traffic benefit. Temporaries that pass only between the fused operators are never allocated in HBM, so they add nothing to peak memory, while any value a backward rule reads must still be stored.20 Rather than executing matrix multiplication, bias addition, and activation functions as separate kernels that serialize intermediate tensors to accelerator global memory (HBM), compilers fuse compatible operators into a single kernel that passes intermediate values through registers or on-chip shared memory.

20 Fusion and saved state: Fusion removes the forward round-trip of an intermediate through HBM, but it does not remove the backward pass’s need for that value. If a derivative rule reads a fused intermediate, such as the pre-activation whose sign forms the ReLU mask, the fused kernel must still write that value (or a compact mask) to memory, or the backward pass must recompute it. Fusion and checkpointing therefore interact, because the compiler decides which intermediates to materialize from what the backward graph will read.

Chetlur, Sharan, Cliff Woolley, Philippe Vandermersch, Jonathan Cohen, John Tran, Bryan Catanzaro, and Evan Shelhamer. 2014. “cuDNN: Efficient Primitives for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1410.0759.

The backward pass itself relies on specialized hardware-optimized kernels. Rather than synthesizing backward passes from naive elementary operations, frameworks invoke tuned backward kernels (such as cuDNN primitives) that optimize memory access strides and register usage on accelerator hardware (Chetlur et al. 2014). Combining checkpointing, kernel fusion, and specialized backward kernels enables large architectures to train within physical accelerator memory limits without incurring out-of-memory faults.

Framework implementation of automatic differentiation

Checkpointing, fusion, and specialized kernels address the physical bottlenecks of automatic differentiation, but production frameworks encapsulate these mechanisms beneath high-level training loops. A standard PyTorch step—optimizer.zero_grad(), forward pass, loss.backward(), and optimizer.step()—appears as four sequential calls. Behind this interface, the runtime builds and traverses dynamic computation graphs, allocates and frees intermediate buffers, schedules backward kernels across CUDA streams, and manages numerical precision. The same graph machinery supports second-order derivatives via nested torch.autograd.grad calls and automatic mixed precision via autocast, which selects reduced-precision kernels for eligible operations while retaining higher precision where its policy requires it. Operation precision and the optimizer’s persistent weight-storage recipe are separate choices.

PyTorch autograd internals

The autograd system is the engine that resolves the differentiation problem introduced in section 1.1. Three systems principles govern its design: the data structure that enables efficient gradient computation, the memory cost of maintaining that data structure, and the control mechanisms that production workflows require. Reasoning through these principles clarifies why training memory substantially exceeds inference memory for identical architectures, and how frameworks manage that overhead.

The reverse-linked graph structure

During the forward pass, autograd dynamically constructs a directed acyclic graph of Node (or Function) objects. Each node encapsulates an operator’s backward differentiation logic and retains references to the tensors required to evaluate its vector-Jacobian products. Because edges point backward from outputs toward inputs, the engine initiates topological traversal immediately upon receiving the scalar loss adjoint.

When gradient recording is enabled and an operation has inputs requiring gradients, its output carries a grad_fn referencing the backward Function. Each Function links to antecedent nodes through its next_functions list, forming a chain from the scalar loss back to leaf parameter tensors. Listing 10 inspects this pointer chain for a simple composite computation:

The inspection exposes the traversal path: PowBackward0 (from z = y.pow(2)) links to MulBackward0 (from y = x * 3), which terminates at AccumulateGrad on the leaf parameter tensor x. Leaf tensors are graph sinks where accumulated gradients write directly into the .grad buffer rather than propagating further. The tuple format (Function, index) tracks the specific output index for multi-output operations.

This reverse-linked graph imposes a lifetime constraint: the topology and the forward information required by each backward rule must remain available until that rule executes. Host-side graph metadata (pointers and node descriptors) is distinct from the device tensor buffers it references. Retaining those buffers can consume gigabytes of accelerator capacity as model depth and batch size grow; recomputation or offloading changes where and how the required values are made available.

The memory-compute trade-off

In a retained-activation implementation, saved device tensors remain live until their backward consumers finish. These retained activations represent a primary driver of training memory overhead, compounding the footprint of weights, gradients, and optimizer states. Because different operations require different forward information—for example, a matrix multiplication weight gradient requires input activations, while elementwise ReLU needs only the pre-activation sign—the exact memory profile depends on operator semantics and how the implementation stores that information.

The training memory sum dwarfs FP32 weight storage alone.

Consider an illustrative ResNet-50 training scenario with 25.6M parameters (~102.4 MB of FP32 weights), batch size 64, and \(224{\times}224\) images. With 8 GB–12 GB of saved activations, gradients add ~102.4 MB, and two Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) FP32 moment buffers add ~204.8 MB. Summing those four components gives an 8.4 GB–12.4 GB training scenario, roughly 82.1–121.2× the FP32 weight storage alone. This is a capacity-planning sum; actual peak memory depends on which components are live together. Inference requires no optimizer states, gradients, or saved backward buffers, but still has live activations and runtime workspace.

This disparity underscores why accelerator training capacity cannot be deduced from parameter counts alone. Total training memory obeys the four-component formulation (\(\TotalMemoryFootprint = \WeightMemory + \GradientMemory + \OptimizerStateMemory + \ActivationMemory\)) plus allocator overhead, as stated in The true cost of training memory.

Frameworks provide three graph-level controls to manage this trade-off. Activation checkpointing (Chen et al. 2016), whose runtime contract listing 9 showed, alters what the graph retains: the engine stores selected segment boundaries and regenerates intermediate activations during the backward pass. In iron law terms, checkpointing increases the arithmetic operation count (\(\Operations\)) to reduce peak activation storage; its effect on transfer volume (\(\DataVolume\)) depends on the recomputation path. Tensor detachment sets a gradient boundary: calling .detach() makes subsequent operations treat that tensor as independent of its prior computation. It does not retroactively free all previously saved state, and cutting a path changes which gradients are computed. Transfer learning shows the payoff. When a frozen feature extractor runs with gradient recording disabled and its output enters the trainable head as a constant, autograd saves none of the extractor’s activations, so only the head pays the activation-storage cost. Mixed-precision training provides a third lever: representing eligible activations and weights in 16-bit formats halves their bytes relative to FP32, reducing tensor volume (\(\DataVolume\)) and enabling higher arithmetic throughput on supported Tensor Cores (\(\PeakRate\)).

Chen, Tianqi, Bing Xu, Chiyuan Zhang, and Carlos Guestrin. 2016. “Training Deep Nets with Sublinear Memory Cost.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1604.06174.

↪ The decision of when these single-machine levers stop being enough and training must scale beyond one machine is developed in When to scale: The physical ceiling.

Extensibility and control

Production training systems require fine-grained control over gradient propagation beyond standard backward sweeps. In practice, workloads demand three core capabilities: selective gradient computation for freezing layers during transfer learning via requires_grad=False or .detach(); gradient inspection and modification for clipping, debugging, or logging intermediate adjoints via runtime hooks; and custom differentiation rules for non-standard operations such as specialized CUDA kernels or novel activation functions.

These extension points operate via callbacks invoked during graph construction and reverse traversal, preserving the core engine’s execution path. In PyTorch, these capabilities map to specific runtime mechanisms: retained graphs, gradient accumulation buffers, custom autograd.Function implementations, tensor hooks, and detachment boundaries. Table 5 summarizes these mechanisms, their systems function, and their memory or correctness trade-offs.

Custom autograd functions expose the differentiation contract directly by transferring state retention responsibility from the engine to the programmer: the implementation explicitly defines which forward tensors to preserve in ctx.save_for_backward() and evaluates the corresponding vector-Jacobian products in backward(). Listing 11 demonstrates this interface for a custom multiply-add operation:

These autograd mechanisms connect directly to the silicon contract. The reverse-linked graph defines the sequence of backward kernels the device must execute (the \(\Operations\) term).
